Improved L 2 L^{2} -error estimates for the wave equation discretized using hybrid nonconforming methods on simplicial meshes Thanks: This project has received funding from the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant agreement No 101034255.
Bernardo Cockburn, Alexandre Ern, Rekha Khot

Abstract
We present improved L 2 L^{2} -error estimates on the time-integrated primal variable for the wave equation in its first-order formulation. The space discretization relies on a hybrid nonconforming method, such as the hybridizable discontinuous Galerkin, the hybrid high-order or the weak Galerkin methods. We consider both equal-order and mixed-order settings on simplices, and include the lowest-order case with piecewise constant unknowns on the faces and in the cells. Our main result is a superclose, resp., optimal bound on the above error in the equal-, resp., mixed-order case. A key result of independent interest to achieve these estimates are novel approximation estimates for an interpolation operator inspired from the hybridizable discontinuous Galerkin literature.



Mathematics Subject Classification:
35L05, 65M15, 65M60.

Keywords:
Wave equation, hybrid high-order, hybridizable discontinuous Galerkin, weak Galerkin, interpolation operator, superconvergence.

1
Introduction

The wave equation is a classical example of hyperbolic partial differential equation.
We focus here on the first-order formulation in space and time, with a skew-symmetric differential operator in space, having the structure of a time-dependent Friedrichs’s system. The first-order formulation is attractive since it makes the application of high-order time discretization schemes such as Runge–Kutta (RK) schemes rather straightforward. It is also more natural than the second-order formulation in time in the context of more complex models derived from conservation laws. The space discretization of Friedrichs’s systems is usually done using discontinuous Galerkin (dG) methods or stabilized
H^{1}
-conforming finite elements (see, e.g.,
[
20
,
16
]
and
[
19
, Chap. 58–60]
for an overview). In the time-dependent case, the combination of RK and dG methods has become a popular paradigm, in the wake of
[
9
]
. See also
[
19
, Chapter 77-78]
for further insight.

Hybrid nonconforming discretization schemes in space are based on approximating the unknowns inside the mesh cells as well as their trace on the mesh faces. The main advantage of such methods with respect to dG is their reduced computational cost owing to static condensation, which eliminates locally all the cell degrees of freedom. The most popular hybrid nonconforming method is the hybridized discontinuous Galerkin (HDG) method introduced in
[
12
]
. A different devising viewpoint was developed some years later, leading to the hybrid high-order (HHO) and weak Galerkin (WG) methods. Both methods were developed independently, the former was coined in
[
13
,
14
]
and the latter in
[
27
]
. In a nutshell, HDG methods approximate a triple composed of the solution, its gradient and its traces on the mesh skeleton and the key devising concept is the numerical flux trace, whereas HHO and WG approximate a pair composed of the solution and its traces on the mesh skeleton and the key devising concepts are local gradient reconstruction and local stabilization operators (the gradient reconstruction operator being called weak gradient in WG). We refer the reader to
[
5
,
4
]
and
[
10
, Subsection 6.6]
for more details on the connections between HDG/HHO/WG methods.
Hybrid nonconforming methods have already been employed to approximate the wave equation in its first-order form. The HDG method has been studied in
[
8
,
26
,
6
]
(see also formulations exploiting the Hamiltonian structure of the equations to combine HDG space discretizations with symplectic time-marching methods
[
23
,
24
,
25
,
11
]
), the HHO method in
[
3
,
2
]
(see also
[
17
]
),
and the WG method in
[
29
,
28
]
.

The main contribution of the paper is a superconvergent
L^{2}
-error estimate on the time-integrated primal variable. The presentation uses the HHO/WG formalism based on a pair of discrete unknowns, but
we briefly indicate the connection to the HDG formalism based on a triple of discrete unknowns.
Our analysis covers both equal-order and mixed-order cases, whereby the polynomial degree of the cell unknowns is the same or one order higher, respectively, than the degree of the face unknowns,

k\geq 0
. In both cases, the polynomial degree of the gradient reconstruction is the same as the degree of the face unknowns. The stabilization is scaled by the reciprocal of the mesh size, and employs the high-order HHO correction in the equal-order case
[
14
]
, whereas it coincides with the Lehrenfeld–Schöberl HDG stabilization in the mixed-order case
[
21
]
. In all cases, provided the exact solution has optimal smoothness, we prove that the decay rate on the
L^{2}
-error on the time-integrated primal variable is
\mathcal{O}(h^{k+1+s})
, where
h
is the mesh size and
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
is the index of elliptic regularity pickup.

To appreciate the improvement brought by our analysis, we summarize in Table
1.1
the decay rates available on the error. The energy error, that is, the
L^{2}
-error on both primal and dual variables, decays at rate
\mathcal{O}(h^{k+1})

[
2
]
. For the dual variable, this rate is indeed optimal, whereas, for the primal variable, this rate is optimal in the equal-order case and suboptimal in the mixed-order case. Here, we improve the estimate on the primal variable by considering its time integration. Indeed, we show that for this time-integrated variable, the decay rate is
\mathcal{O}(h^{k+1+s})
, which means a supercloseness estimate in the equal-order case and an optimal estimate in the mixed-order case.
The idea of considering a time-integrated variable comes from

[
8
]
, where an improved
L^{2}
-error estimate is shown using equal-order HDG and plain Least-Squares stabilization. The main differences with the present work are that
[
8
]
assumes full elliptic regularity and
k\geq 1
, and requires a local post-processing using the discrete dual variable. Here, we consider more general stabilization strategies, any elliptic regularity pickup
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, include the case
k=0
, and do not require using the discrete dual variable.

)

)

)

Table 1.1
:
Convergence rates of energy and
L^{2}
-errors in equal- and mixed-order settings given a polynomial degree
k\geq 0
. In all cases, the face polynomial degree for the primal variable is
k
.

Our convergence analysis hinges on an auxiliary result of independent and broader interest, namely an interpolation operator with supercloseness (equal-order) or optimal (mixed-order) approximation properties.
This operator in the mixed-order case is similar to the so-called HDG+-interpolation operator
[
15
]
(see also the HDG-interpolation operator from
[
7
]
). In the equal-order case, this operator is, to our knowledge, novel. Moreover, some of the arguments in the proof of its approximation estimates are slightly simpler than those in
[
7
]
. Another relevant improvement in the analysis is that we relax the regularity requirement on the divergence of the dual variable, which is important if one wants to address the case of partial elliptic regularity pickup (less than 1). We call our novel operator the H-interpolation operator, and hope that it will find further applications in the analysis of HDG/HHO/WG methods. In the present setting, the main role of the H-interpolation operator lies in the handling of the initial conditions on the time derivatives.

The paper is organized as follows: Section
2
introduces the model problem and the HDG/HHO/WG discretization. Section
3
collects our main results on the H-interpolation operator and on the error estimates for the wave equation. Section
4
is devoted to all preliminary results needed in Section
5
to establish the well-posedness of the H-interpolation operator and the approximation estimates. Finally, Section
6
contains the error analysis, first in the energy norm and then in the
L^{2}
-norm for the time-integrated primal variable.
For illustrative numerical experiments related to our theoretical results, we refer the reader to
[
3
,
22
]
.

2
Model problem and space semi-discretization

This section presents the model problem and its space semi-discretization using hybrid nonconforming methods.

2.1
Model problem

Let
\Omega
be a polyhedral Lipschitz domain (open bounded connected set) in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d\in\{2,3\}
with boundary
\Gamma
. The first-order formulation of the acoustic wave equation defined on the space domain
\Omega
and the time domain
J:=(0,T_{f})
for the final time
T_{f}>0
consists of the coupled partial differential equations in
J\times\Omega
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\nabla v

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0},

(2.1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v-\nabla{{\cdot}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle=f,

(2.1b)

involving as unknowns the dual variable
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:J\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the primal variable
v:J\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, and the known source term
f:J\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
. The initial conditions are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}(0)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0},\quad v(0)=v_{0}\quad\text{in}\;\Omega,

with given data
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
v_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, and the boundary condition is (for simplicity)

\displaystyle v=0\quad\text{on}\;J\times\Gamma.

We follow the standard notation for Sobolev/Lebesgue spaces and respective norms. The
L^{2}
-inner product and the associated norm on a domain
S
are denoted by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{S}
and
\|\cdot\|_{S}
respectively. We use a bold symbol for vector variables and spaces composed of vector-valued fields, e.g.,
\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(\Omega):=[L^{2}(\Omega)]^{d}
.

We set
\underline{V}:=\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla{\cdot},\Omega)\times H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\subset\underline{L}:=\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)
. Here and in what follows, we underline symbols that refer to a pair composed of one dual variable and one primal variable. Assuming an initial condition
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0},v_{0})\in\underline{V}
, the Hille–Yosida theorem gives a solution
(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in C^{0}(\overline{J};\underline{V})\cap C^{1}(\overline{J};\underline{L})
. In particular, we have, for all
t\in\overline{J}
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi})_{\Omega}-(\nabla v(t),\boldsymbol{\xi})_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(\Omega),

(2.2a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v(t),w)_{\Omega}+(\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t),\nabla w)_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=(f(t),w)_{\Omega}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle w\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega).

(2.2b)

Below, we use this weak formulation to define our space semi-discretization schemes.

2.2
Space semi-discretization

This section presents the HHO/WG space semi-discretization of the model problem and its equivalent HDG rewriting.

2.2.1
Mesh and polynomial spaces

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a simplicial mesh covering exactly the domain
\Omega
. The set
\mathcal{F}
contains the mesh faces, and is divided into the set of mesh interfaces
\mathcal{F}^{\circ}
and the set of mesh boundary faces
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
. A generic mesh cell is denoted by
T\in\mathcal{T}
with diameter
h_{T}
, unit outward normal
\boldsymbol{n}_{T}
on
\partial T
, and the set
\mathcal{F}_{\partial T}
collects the mesh faces located on the boundary of
T
.

Let
k\geq 0
be the polynomial degree and let
k^{\prime}\in\{k,k+1\}
. We set

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}:=\bigtimes_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{T}^{k},

with
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{T}^{k}:=\mathbb{P}^{k}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as well as

\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}:=V_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}\times V_{\mathcal{F}}^{k},\qquad V_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}:=\bigtimes_{T\in\mathcal{T}}V_{T}^{k^{\prime}},\qquad V_{\mathcal{F}}^{k}:=\bigtimes_{F\in\mathcal{F}}V_{F}^{k},

where
V_{T}^{k^{\prime}}:=\mathbb{P}^{k^{\prime}}(T;\mathbb{R})
(resp.
V_{F}^{k}:=\mathbb{P}^{k}(F;\mathbb{R})
) is composed of the scalar-valued
d
-variate (resp.
(d-1)
-variate) polynomials of degree at most
k^{\prime}
(resp.
k
) restricted to the cell
T
(resp. face
F
).
Here, we use the subscript
\mathcal{M}:=(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F})
to indicate the joint collection of mesh cells and faces. For a generic
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
and a generic
\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}\in\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}
, we write

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}}:=(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T})_{T\in\mathcal{T}},\qquad\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}:=(w_{\mathcal{T}},w_{\mathcal{F}}):=\big((w_{T})_{T\in\mathcal{T}},(w_{F})_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\big),\qquad w_{\partial T}:=(w_{F})_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\partial T}}.

In what follows, we use a hat symbol, as in
\hat{v}
, to refer to a pair composed of a cell unknown
v_{T}
, and a face unknown,
v_{\partial T}
. To impose the zero Dirichlet boundary condition on the primal variable, we define
\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}:=V_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}\times V_{\mathcal{F}0}^{k}
with
V_{\mathcal{F}0}^{k}:=\{v_{\mathcal{F}}\in V_{\mathcal{F}}^{k}:v_{F}=0\quad\forall F\in\mathcal{F}^{\partial}\}
. Let
\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}
(resp.
\Pi^{k}_{F}
) be the
L^{2}(T)
-orthogonal (resp.
L^{2}(F)
-orthogonal) projection onto
V_{T}^{k^{\prime}}
(resp.
V^{k}_{F}
). Let
\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{T}}
(resp.
\Pi^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}
) be the piecewise
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto
V_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}
(resp.
V^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}
). Recall the following approximation estimate: For all
T\in\mathcal{T}
and all
v\in H^{m}(T)
with
m\in\{0{:}k^{\prime}\}
,

\displaystyle\lVert v-\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}(v)\rVert_{H^{r}(T)}\lesssim h_{T}^{m-r}|v|_{H^{m}(T)}\qquad\forall r\in\{0{:}m\}.

(2.3)

Similar definitions and estimates hold for vector-valued functions. We also define the elliptic projection
{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}:H^{1}(T)\to\mathbb{P}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\nabla({\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v)),\nabla q)_{T}

\displaystyle=(\nabla v,\nabla q)_{T}\qquad\forall q\in\mathbb{P}^{k+1}_{*}(T;\mathbb{R}):=\mathbb{P}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R},

(2.4a)

\displaystyle({\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v),1)_{T}

\displaystyle=(v,1)_{T},

(2.4b)

which satisfies the following approximation estimate:

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T}\lesssim h_{T}^{k+1}|v|_{H^{k+1}(T)}.

(2.5)

2.2.2
HHO/WG space semi-discretization

We approximate the primal variable
v
with an HHO/WG method using cell polynomials of degree
k^{\prime}
and face polynomials of degree
k
. The setting is said to be of equal-order if
k^{\prime}=k
and of mixed-order if
k^{\prime}=k+1
. On the other hand, we approximate the dual variable
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
with a dG method using cell polynomials of degree
k
. The stabilization is the one introduced in HHO methods for the equal-order case and in HDG methods for the mixed-order case, whereas WG methods in the equal-order case generally employ a plain Least-Squares stabilization (see Remark
2.1
).

The space semi-discretized wave equation reads as follows: Find
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}:\overline{J}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
and
\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}:\overline{J}\rightarrow\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
such that, for all
t\in\overline{J}
, all
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
and all
\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}\in\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}-(\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}(t)),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.6a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v_{\mathcal{T}}(t),w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}+(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}(t),\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}))_{\Omega}+s_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}(t),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})

\displaystyle=(f,w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega},

(2.6b)

where the gradient reconstruction operator
\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}:\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Sigma}_{T}^{k}
is such that, for all
\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}\in\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}
and all
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{T}^{k}
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}:={}

\displaystyle\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\big\{-(v_{T},\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T})_{T}+(v_{\partial T},\boldsymbol{\xi}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T})_{\partial T}\big\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\big\{(\nabla v_{T},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T})_{T}-(v_{T}-v_{\partial T},\boldsymbol{\xi}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T})_{\partial T}\big\}.

(2.7)

The stabilization bilinear form
s_{\mathcal{M}}:\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is such that

s_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}},\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}):=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\tau_{T}\big(S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(v_{T},v_{\partial T}),S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(w_{T},w_{\partial T})\big)_{\partial T},\quad\text{with}\quad\tau_{T}:=\ell_{\Omega}h_{T}^{-1},

(2.8)

where the scaling factor
\ell_{\Omega}:=\text{diam}(\Omega)
is introduced to make
the weight
\tau_{T}
non-dimensional,
and the operator
S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}
is defined as

2

for
k’=k+1,

with the boundary difference operator

\displaystyle\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{v}_{T}):=v_{T}|_{\partial T}-v_{\partial T}.

(2.10)

Here,
\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto the space
\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R}):=\bigtimes_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{T}}V_{F}^{k}
so that
S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\hat{v}_{T})\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R})
in both cases. Moreover, the local potential reconstruction operator
R_{T}:\hat{V}_{T}^{k}\to\mathbb{P}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R})
is defined, for all
\hat{v}_{T}\in\hat{V}_{T}^{k}
, such that

\displaystyle(\nabla R_{T}(\hat{v}_{T}),\nabla w)_{T}

\displaystyle=(\nabla v_{T},\nabla w)_{T}-(v_{T}-v_{\partial T},\nabla w{\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T})_{\partial T}\qquad\forall w\in\mathbb{P}^{k+1}_{\mbox{*}}(T;\mathbb{R}),

(2.11a)

\displaystyle(R_{T}(\hat{v}_{T})-v_{T},1)_{T}

\displaystyle=0.

(2.11b)

In (
2.9
), we notice that the correction term involving the operator
R_{T}
is added to the plain least-squares stabilization classically considered in the context of dG methods.
Finally, (
2.6
) is initialized by prescribing

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}(0):=\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0},v_{0}),\qquad v_{\mathcal{T}}(0):=\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0},v_{0}),

(2.12)

where the projections
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}},\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}})
are yet to be defined.

Remark 2.1

(Role of stabilization)
.

Taking the stabilization operator equal to the difference operator
\delta_{\partial T}
corresponds to plain Least-Squares stabilization. Here, we consider more sophisticated choices that play a key role in achieving higher-order estimates. In particular, the stabilization operator enters the definition of the H-interpolation operator (see (
2.17c
) below). This choice, in turn, is instrumental in achieving improved approximation estimates on the primal variable (see (
3.1a
) and Remark
3.3
below).

2.2.3
HDG rewriting

The discrete problem (
2.6
) can be recast in the HDG setting, see
[
3
, Section 4.3]
and
[
5
]
for details. Here, we recall the local discrete problem which is obtained by taking test functions with support localized in the mesh cell
T\in\mathcal{T}
, using the definition of
\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}
in (
2.6a
)-(
2.6b
), and rewriting the stabilization term in (
2.6b
). Notice from the definition of the operator
R_{T}
that

\displaystyle R_{T}(\hat{v}_{T})=R_{T}(v_{T},v_{T}|_{\partial T})-R_{T}(0,\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{v}_{T}))=v_{T}-R_{T}(0,\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{v}_{T})).

(2.13)

Hence, we can rewrite
S_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}(\hat{v}_{T})=\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}\{\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{v}_{T})-(1-\Pi^{k}_{T})R_{T}(0,\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{v}_{T}))|_{\partial T}\}
. This together with the definition of
S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}
shows that in both cases
S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}
only acts on
\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{v}_{T})
. Thus, we define the operator
\widetilde{S}_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}:\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R})
by

2

~

S

_∂T^eo(
μ
):=Π^k_∂T
{
μ
-((1-Π^k_T)R_T(0,
μ
))—_∂T}
for
k’=k,

~

_∂T^mo(

μ
}
for
k’=k+1.

For all
T\in\mathcal{T}
and all
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T},w_{T})\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{T}^{k}\times V_{T}^{k^{\prime}}
, we infer from (
2.6
) that

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{T}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T})_{T}+(v_{T}(t),\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T})_{T}-(v_{\partial T}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{T}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T})_{\partial T}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.15a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v_{T}(t),w_{T})_{T}+(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{T}(t),\nabla w_{T})_{T}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\partial T}(t),w_{T})_{\partial T}

\displaystyle=(f(t),w_{T})_{T}.

(2.15b)

For all
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}
with
F\in\partial T^{-}\cap\partial T^{+}
and all
w_{F}\in V_{F}^{k}
, we infer from (
2.6b
) that

\displaystyle(\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\partial T^{-}}(t)+\hat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\partial T^{+}}(t),w_{F})_{F}=0,

(3.13c)

with the numerical flux trace
2
((

denotes the adjoint operator of
\widetilde{S}_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}
with the respect to
L^{2}(\partial T)
-inner product.

Remark 2.2

(Initial face values)
.

v_{\mathcal{F}}(0)

2.12
), but results from
(

, and defining

\{\!\!\{\phi\}\!\!\}_{F}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\phi|_{T^{-}}|_{F}+\phi|_{T^{+}}|_{F}\big),\qquad[\![\phi]\!]_{F}:=\phi|_{T^{-}}|_{F}-\phi|_{T^{+}}|_{F},

.

)

\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R})
, we have

(\mu,{\lambda_{\partial T}(\mu)})_{\partial T}=(\mu,{((\widetilde{S}_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}})^{*}\circ\widetilde{S}_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}})(\mu)})_{\partial T}=\lVert\widetilde{S}_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}(\mu)\rVert^{2}_{\partial T}=\lVert S_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}(0,\mu)\rVert^{2}_{\partial T}\gtrsim\lVert\mu\rVert^{2}_{\partial T},

where the lower bound follows from the stability estimate of Lemma
4.3
below with
\hat{v}_{T}:=(0,\mu)
. In particular, the above bound shows that
\lambda_{\partial T}^{-1}
is well-defined.

2.3
The H-interpolation operator

In this section, we introduce our novel, key tool for the error analysis, namely, the H-interpolation operator. We consider both equal- and mixed-order settings.
We first provide a unified definition of the H-interpolation operator, and then discuss separately the equal- and mixed-order cases.

Unified H-interpolation operator
. Let
T\in\mathcal{T}
. For all
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\times H^{\nu_{v}}(T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
\nu_{v}>\frac{1}{2}
, the H-interpolation operator

\displaystyle\underline{\Pi}^{\textsc{h}}_{T}(\underline{v}):=(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}))\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{P}^{k^{\prime}}(T;\mathbb{R})

on any simplex
T\in\mathcal{T}
is defined by the following equations:

\displaystyle(\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-v,w)_{T}=0

\displaystyle\forall w\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}),

(2.17a)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\xi})_{T}=0

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.17b)

\displaystyle((\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\mu)_{\partial T}=\tau_{T}(S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(v|_{\partial T})),S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(0,\mu))_{\partial T}

\displaystyle\forall\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R}),

(2.17c)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\nabla q)_{T}=((\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\partial T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\partial T})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},q)_{\partial T}

\displaystyle\forall q\in\mathbb{P}^{k^{\prime}}(T;\mathbb{R}),

(2.17d)

recalling the convention that
\mathbb{P}^{-1}(T;\mathbb{R})=\{0\}
and
\mathbb{P}^{-1}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{\mathbb{0}\}
. Recall also that
S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}:=S_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}
for
k^{\prime}=k
and
S_{\partial T}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}:=S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}
for
k^{\prime}=k+1
in (
2.17c
). Moreover, since (
2.17b
) and the
L^{2}
-orthogonality of
\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}
imply that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\nabla q)_{T}=0=((\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\partial T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\partial T})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},q)_{\partial T}\qquad\forall q\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(T;\mathbb{R}),

the equation (
2.17d
) is redundant for
k^{\prime}=k
, and
reduces to taking
q
in the linear space
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R})
composed of all homogeneous polynomials of degree (
k+1
) for
k^{\prime}=k+1
. Altogether, we can rewrite the explicit definitions in the equal- and mixed-order cases as follows:

Equal-order case
. For
k^{\prime}=k
, the H-interpolation operator from (
2.17
) is defined on any simplex
T\in\mathcal{T}
as follows:

\displaystyle(\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-v,w)_{T}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle w\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}),

(2.18a)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\xi})_{T}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.18b)

\displaystyle((\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\mu)_{\partial T}

\displaystyle=\tau_{T}(S_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}(\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(v|_{\partial T})),S_{\partial T}^{\rm{eo}}(0,\mu))_{\partial T}\quad

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R}).

(2.18c)

Mixed-order case
. For
k^{\prime}=k+1
, the H-interpolation operator from (
2.17
) is defined on any simplex
T\in\mathcal{T}
as follows:

\displaystyle(\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-v,w)_{T}=0

\displaystyle\forall w\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}),

(2.19a)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\xi})_{T}=0

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.19b)

\displaystyle((\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\mu)_{\partial T}=\tau_{T}(S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(v|_{\partial T})),S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(0,\mu))_{\partial T}

\displaystyle\forall\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R}),

(2.19c)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\nabla\tilde{q})_{T}=((\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\partial T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\partial T})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\tilde{q})_{\partial T}

\displaystyle\forall\tilde{q}\in\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R}).

(2.19d)

The first three equations (
2.19a
)-(
2.19c
) are formally the same as in the equal-order case, see (
2.18a
)-(
2.18c
) (but the definition of the stabilization operator changes) and one more equation, (
2.19d
), is added because of the additional degrees of freedom in the mixed-order case.

Remark 2.3

(Comparison with
[
15
]
)
.

Let
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in(\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\cap\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T))\times H^{\nu_{v}}(T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
\nu_{v}>\frac{1}{2}
. In the mixed-order case, the action of the
\rm{H}
-interpolation operator gives the same result as the operator
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T},\widetilde{\Pi}^{v}_{T})
, defined in
[
15
, Chapter 4]
as follows:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\Pi}^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-v,w)_{T}=0

\displaystyle\forall w\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}),

(2.20a)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\xi})_{T}=0

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{P}^{k-1}(T;\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.20b)

\displaystyle((\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\mu)_{\partial T}=\tau_{T}(S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(v|_{\partial T})),S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(0,\mu))_{\partial T}

\displaystyle\forall\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\partial T};\mathbb{R}),

(2.20c)

\displaystyle(\nabla{\cdot}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\tilde{q})_{T}=\tau_{T}(S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(v|_{\partial T})),S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(\tilde{q},0))_{\partial T}

\displaystyle\forall\tilde{q}\in\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R}).

(2.20d)

Indeed, for all
\tilde{q}\in\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{k+1}(T;\mathbb{R})
, using (
2.20d
) and
S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(\tilde{q},0)=-S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(0,\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(\tilde{q}|_{\partial T}))
owing to (
4.4
), we infer that

\displaystyle(\nabla{\cdot}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\tilde{q})_{T}

\displaystyle=-\tau_{T}(S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{v}_{T}(\underline{v}),\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(v|_{\partial T})),S_{\partial T}^{\rm{mo}}(0,\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(\tilde{q}|_{\partial T})))_{\partial T}

\displaystyle=((\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(\tilde{q}|_{\partial T}))_{\partial T},

(2.21)

invoking (
2.20c
) with
\mu:=-\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}(\tilde{q}|_{\partial T})
in the last step.
An integration by parts, (
2.21
), and the
L^{2}
-orthogonality of
\Pi^{k}_{\partial T}
lead to

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma},\nabla\tilde{q})_{T}

\displaystyle=-(\nabla{\cdot}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\tilde{q})_{T}+((\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\tilde{q})_{\partial T}

\displaystyle=((\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\partial T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\partial T})-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\tilde{q})_{\partial T}+((\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\tilde{q})_{\partial T}

\displaystyle=((\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\partial T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\partial T})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},\tilde{q})_{\partial T}.

(2.22)

Altogether, the identities (
2.20a
)-(
2.20c
) and (
2.22
) imply that
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}_{T}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\underline{v}),\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}^{v}(\underline{v}))
satisfies the equations (
2.19
). Since (
2.19
) has a unique solution owing to Lemma
3.1
below, we infer that
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}{}_{T}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\underline{v}),\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}^{v}(\underline{v}))=(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{T}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\underline{v}),\Pi_{T}^{v}(\underline{v}))
. Notice though that the definition (
2.20
) needs
\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T)
regularity, which is not needed in the definition (
2.19
).

3
Main results

In this section, we present and comment the main results of the paper.

3.1
Main results on the H-interpolation operator

We show that the H-interpolation operator is well defined and satisfies superclose or optimal approximation estimates. Finally, we relax the regularity assumption on the divergence of the dual variable. The proofs of the results stated in this section are postponed to Section
5
.

3.1.1
Well-posedness and approximation estimates

Lemma 3.1

(Well-posedness)
.

For all
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\times H^{\nu_{v}}(T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
\nu_{v}>\frac{1}{2}
, the
\rm{H}
-interpolation operator
\underline{\Pi}^{\textsc{h}}_{T}
is well-defined.

Theorem 3.2

(Approximation estimates)
.

For all
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in(\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\cap\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T))\times H^{1}(T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\lVert\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}(v)\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell_{\Omega}^{-1}h_{T}^{2}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}+h_{T}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T},

(3.1a)

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim h_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T}\rVert_{\partial T}+h_{T}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\quad+\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T},

(3.1b)

with the convention that
\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=0
for
k=0
.

Remark 3.3

(Supercloseness/optimality)
.

Theorem
3.2
gives a supercloseness (resp. optimal) estimate for the primal variable in the equal- (resp. mixed-order) case, and an optimal estimate for the dual variable in both equal- and mixed-order cases. In particular,
when
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in\boldsymbol{H}^{k+1}(\Omega)\times H^{k+2}(\Omega)
, the estimate (
3.1a
) followed by the classical bounds from (
2.3
) and (
2.5
) gives

\displaystyle\lVert\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}(v)\rVert_{T}\lesssim\ell_{\Omega}^{-1}h_{T}^{k+2}(|\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\boldsymbol{H}^{k+1}(\Omega)}+\ell_{\Omega}|v|_{H^{k+2}(\Omega)}).

This is the key estimate to achieve an improved
L^{2}
-error estimate on the time-integrated primal variable (see Theorem
3.16
below).

Remark 3.4

(Approximation estimates)
.

For all
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in(\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\cap\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T))\times H^{1}(T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
, the triangle inequality and Theorem
3.2
provide the following estimates:

\displaystyle\lVert v-\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert v-\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}(v)\rVert_{T}+\ell^{-1}_{\Omega}h_{T}^{2}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}+h_{T}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T},

(3.2a)

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}+h_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T}\rVert_{\partial T}+h_{T}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\quad+\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T}.

(3.2b)

Remark 3.5

(Separation of primal and dual variables)
.

It is interesting to observe that there is no relation between
v
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
in the definition (
2.17
) and in the estimates (
3.1
) from Theorem
3.2
. Thus, the estimate (
3.1a
) is equivalent to the two estimates

\displaystyle\lVert\Pi^{v}_{T}(\mathbb{0},v)-\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}(v)\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim h_{T}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T},

\displaystyle\lVert\Pi^{v}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma},0)\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell^{-1}_{\Omega}h_{T}^{2}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T},

and the estimate (
3.1b
) to

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma},0)-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim h_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T}\rVert_{\partial T}+h_{T}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T},

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\mathbb{0},v)\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T}.

Remark 3.6

(Relaxed regularity)
.

The approximation estimates in
[
15
, Proposition 4.6]
are proved assuming
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\boldsymbol{H}^{1}(T)
, whereas we only assume
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\cap\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
. We show how to relax the regularity on
\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T)
next.

3.1.2
Extension to weaker regularity of dual variable

Now, we relax the regularity assumption
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla{\cdot};T)
on any simplex
T\in\mathcal{T}
, required in Theorem
3.2
. Set
\boldsymbol{H}^{s-1}(\nabla\cdot;T):=\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(T):\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in H^{s-1}(T)\}
with
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. Notice that, for
s=1
, we have
\boldsymbol{H}^{0}(\nabla\cdot;T)=\boldsymbol{H}(\nabla\cdot;T)
.
We define the dual norm

\displaystyle\|\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\|_{H^{s-1}(T)}:=\sup_{w\in H^{1-s}_{0}(T)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\langle\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma},w\rangle_{T}}{|w|_{H^{1-s}(T)}},

(3.3)

where
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle_{T}
denotes the
duality pairing between
H^{s-1}(T)
and
H_{0}^{1-s}(T)
. Notice that

1-s<\frac{1}{2}
, so that
H^{1-s}_{0}(T)=H^{1-s}(T)
(see e.g.,
[
18
, Theorem 3.19]
). We also
observe that
\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})
remains well-defined even if

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\boldsymbol{H}^{s-1}(\nabla{\cdot};T)
.

Theorem 3.7

(Approximation estimates with negative regularity on
\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
)
.

For all
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in(\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(T)\cap\boldsymbol{H}^{s-1}(\nabla\cdot;T))\times H^{1}(T)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\lVert\Pi^{v}_{T}(\underline{v})-\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{T}(v)\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell_{\Omega}^{-1}h_{T}^{1+s}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{H^{s-1}(T)}+h_{T}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T},

(3.4a)

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}(\underline{v})-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim h_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\boldsymbol{\sigma}){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T}\rVert_{\partial T}+h_{T}^{s}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi^{k-1}_{T}(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{H^{s-1}(T)}

\displaystyle\quad+\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\nabla(v-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(v))\rVert_{T}.

(3.4b)

3.2
A priori error analysis for the wave equation

This section presents the energy and
L^{2}
-error estimates for the wave equation. We notice that our main focus is on the
L^{2}
-error estimate, whose proof does not use the energy-error estimate. We present the latter to showcase the improvement achieved by the former. The energy-error estimate is also of independent interest. The proofs of the results stated in this section are postponed to Section
6
.

3.2.1
Errors and their equations

Let
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in C^{0}(\overline{J};\underline{V})\cap C^{1}(\overline{J};\underline{L})
solve the continuous problem (
2.2
) and let
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}})\in C^{1}(\overline{J};\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k})
solve the discrete problem (
2.6
). Let
\underline{D}^{\textsc{h}}:=\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\Omega)\times H^{\nu_{v}}(\Omega)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
\nu_{v}>\frac{1}{2}
be the domain of the global H-interpolation operator
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}},\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}})
, which is defined componentwise as
\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}}({\cdot})|_{T}:=\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T}({\cdot}|_{T})
and
\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}({\cdot})|_{T}:=\Pi^{v}_{T}({\cdot}|_{T})
for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
with
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{T},\Pi^{v}_{T})
defined by (
2.17
), i.e., by (
2.18
) in the equal-order case and by (
2.19
) in the mixed-order case. Thus,
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}},\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}):\underline{D}^{\textsc{h}}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times V_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}
.

We define the space semi-discrete errors, for all
t\in\overline{J}
, as

H-errors:

\displaystyle\quad\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{h}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}(t)),

\displaystyle\qquad\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\textsc{h}}(t):=\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}(t)-(\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}(t)),\Pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(v(t)|_{\mathcal{F}})),

HHO-errors:

\displaystyle\quad\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\mathcal{T}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)),

\displaystyle\qquad\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t):=\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}}(t)-(\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{T}}(v(t)),\Pi^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(v(t)|_{\mathcal{F}})),

where
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\mathcal{T}},\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{T}}):\underline{L}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k}_{\mathcal{T}}\times V_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}
are the global
L^{2}
-orthogonal projections.

The error equations associated with the above errors have different properties.
Interestingly, the combination of the two
helps in proving the improved
L^{2}
-error estimates.

Lemma 3.8

(Error equations using H-interpolation)
.

Assume that
\underline{v}\in C^{1}(\overline{J};\underline{D}^{\textsc{h}})
. The following holds: For all
t\in\overline{J}
and all
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{h}}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}-(\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\textsc{h}}(t)),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}=(\partial_{t}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\underline{v}(t))),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega},

(3.5a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}e_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{h}}(t),w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}+(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{h}}(t),\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}))_{\Omega}+s_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\textsc{h}}(t),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})=(\partial_{t}(v(t)-\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}(t))),w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}.

(3.5b)

Lemma 3.9

(Error equations using HHO interpolation)
.

The following holds: For all
t\in\overline{J}
and all
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}-(\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t)),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}=0,

(3.6a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}e_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t),w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}+(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t),\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}))_{\Omega}+s_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})=\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{v}(t);\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}),

(3.6b)

where the linear form
\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{z};\cdot):\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined, for all
\underline{z}:=(\boldsymbol{\zeta},z)\in\underline{V}
and
\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}\in\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
, as

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{z};\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}):=-(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\zeta},w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}-(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\mathcal{T}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(t)),\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}))_{\Omega}-s_{\mathcal{M}}((\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{T}}(z),\Pi^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(z(t)|_{\mathcal{F}})),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}).

(3.7)

Corollary 3.10

(Combination of discrete HHO- and H-errors)
.

Assume that
\underline{v}\in C^{1}(\overline{J};\underline{D}^{\textsc{h}})
. The following holds: For all
t\in\overline{J}
and all
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}-(\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\textsc{h}}(t)),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}=0,

(3.8a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}e_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t),w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}+(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{h}}(t),\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}))_{\Omega}+s_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\textsc{h}}(t),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})=0.

(3.8b)

Corollary 3.11

(Initial error on time-derivatives)
.

The following holds:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(0)=\mathbb{0},\quad\partial_{t}e_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(0)=0.

(3.9)

Remark 3.12

(Reconstructed error gradient)
.

The equations (
3.6a
) and (
3.8a
) imply that
\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(t))=\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{e}^{\textsc{h}}_{\mathcal{M}}(t))
for all
t\in\overline{J}
.

Remark 3.13

(Ritz projection)
.

Lemma
3.8
proposes a way to construct a global Ritz projection
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{v}}_{\mathcal{M}})
satisfying the following equations: Given
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in\underline{V}
, find
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}),\hat{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{v}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\underline{v}))\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
such that, for all
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})\in\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k}
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{v}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\underline{v})),\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=(\nabla v,\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}

(3.10a)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}),\boldsymbol{G}_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}))_{\Omega}+s_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{v}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\underline{v})),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})

\displaystyle=-(\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\sigma},w_{\mathcal{T}})_{\Omega}.

(3.10b)

One can verify that
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\underline{v}),\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{v}(\underline{v}),\Pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{k}(v|_{\mathcal{F}}))
satisfies the above equations, which implies the existence of a Ritz projection. However, the uniqueness in (
3.10
) is not guaranteed. Indeed, we have
\hat{\Pi}^{\textsc{r}_{v}}_{\mathcal{M}}(\underline{0})=0,
but
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\underline{0})
may not be zero. We only have
\nabla{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\underline{0})=0
and
\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}^{i}}[\![\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\textsc{r}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\underline{0})]\!]{\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{F}=0
, implying that there may not be an unique solution to (
3.10
).

3.2.2
Error estimates

Let
t\in\widetilde{J}:=\overline{J}\setminus\{0\}=(0,T_{f}]
and set
J_{t}:=(0,t)
. We use the following shorthand notation:

\displaystyle|\psi|_{L^{p}(J_{t};**)}^{{p}}

\displaystyle:=\int_{J_{t}}|\psi(s)|_{**}^{p}\,\mathrm{ds}\quad\text{for}\;p\in[1,\infty),\qquad

\displaystyle|\psi|_{C^{0}(J_{t};**)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{s\in\overline{J_{t}}}|\psi(s)|_{**},

\displaystyle\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{p}(J_{t};**)}^{{p}}

\displaystyle:=\int_{J_{t}}\lVert\psi(s)\rVert_{**}^{p}\,\mathrm{ds}\quad\text{for}\;p\in[1,\infty),\qquad

\displaystyle\lVert\psi\rVert_{C^{0}(J_{t};**)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{s\in\overline{J_{t}}}\lVert\psi(s)\rVert_{**},

where the seminorm
|\cdot|_{**}
and the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{**}
depend on the context.
For an integer number
m\in\{1,2\}
, we also consider the seminorm

|\psi|_{C^{m}(J;**)}:=\sum_{r\in\{0{:}m\}}T_{f}^{r}|\partial_{t}^{r}\psi|_{C^{0}(J;**)}

and the norm
\|\psi\|_{C^{m}(J;**)}:=\sum_{r\in\{0{:}m\}}T_{f}^{r}\|\partial_{t}^{r}\psi\|_{C^{0}(J;**)}

with the convention
\partial_{t}^{0}\psi:=\psi
.

We denote the stabilization seminorm by
|\cdot|_{\textsc{s}}^{2}:=s_{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. For a linear form
\psi:\hat{V}^{k}_{\mathcal{M}0}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the dual norm

\displaystyle\lVert\psi\rVert_{(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}})^{\prime}}:=\sup_{\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}\in\hat{V}^{k}_{\mathcal{M}0}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\psi(\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})}{\lVert\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}\rVert_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}}\quad\text{with}\quad\lVert\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}\rVert_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}^{2}:=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\Big(\lVert\nabla w_{T}\rVert_{T}^{2}+h_{T}^{-1}\lVert\delta_{\partial T}(\hat{w}_{T})\rVert^{2}_{\partial T}\Big),

(3.11)

and recalling the linear form
\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{z};\cdot):\hat{V}^{k}_{\mathcal{M}0}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\underline{z}\in\underline{V}
defined in (
3.7
), we define for all
\underline{v}\in C^{0}(\overline{J};\underline{V})
and all
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lVert\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{v};\cdot)\rVert_{L^{p}(J_{t};(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}})^{\prime})}^{{p}}

\displaystyle:=\int_{J_{t}}\lVert\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{v}(s);\cdot)\rVert_{(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}})^{\prime}}^{p}\,\mathrm{ds},\;

\displaystyle\lVert\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{v};\cdot)\rVert_{C^{0}(J_{t};(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}})^{\prime})}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{s\in\overline{J_{t}}}\lVert\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{v}(s);\cdot)\rVert_{(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}})^{\prime}}.

For dimensional consistency, we keep track of the dependency of the constants on
T_{f}
and
\ell_{\Omega}
, but we hide the non-dimensional ratio
\ell_{\Omega}/T_{f}
in the constants.

Theorem 3.14

(Optimal energy-error estimate)
.

Assume that
\underline{v}_{0}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0},v_{0})\in\underline{D}^{\textsc{h}}
. Let
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in C^{0}(\overline{J};\underline{V})\cap C^{1}(\overline{J};\underline{L})

solve the continuous problem (
2.2
) and let
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\mathcal{M}})\in C^{1}(\overline{J};\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}\times\hat{V}_{\mathcal{M}0}^{k})
solve the discrete problem (
2.6
) with the initial condition

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathcal{T}}(0)=\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}_{0}),\quad v_{\mathcal{T}}(0)=\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}_{0})\quad\text{with}\quad\underline{v}_{0}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{0},v_{0}).

Remark 3.15

(Convergence rates)
.

Assuming
\underline{z}:=(\boldsymbol{\zeta},z)\in\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\Omega)\times H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}>\frac{1}{2}
, the consistency error
\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}
defined in (
3.7
) can be further simplified as

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{z};\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}})=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}((\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T},w_{\partial T}-w_{T})_{\partial T}-s_{\mathcal{M}}((\Pi^{k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{T}}(z),\Pi^{k}_{\mathcal{F}}(z|_{\mathcal{F}})),\hat{w}_{\mathcal{M}}),

and one can show that

\lVert\psi_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\underline{z};\cdot)\rVert_{(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}})^{\prime}}\lesssim\Big\{\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}h_{T}\lVert(\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})){\cdot}\boldsymbol{n}_{T}\rVert_{\partial T}^{2}+\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\nabla(z-{\cal{E}}_{T}^{k+1}(z))\rVert_{T}^{2}\Big\}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence, if
\underline{v}:=(\boldsymbol{\sigma},v)\in C^{1}(\overline{J};\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\Omega)\times H^{1+\nu_{v}}(\Omega))
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\nu_{v}\in(\frac{1}{2},k+1]
, we have

\lVert e_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}\rVert_{C^{0}(J_{t};\Omega)}+\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{C^{0}(J_{t};\Omega)}+|\hat{e}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\mathcal{M}}|_{L^{2}(J_{t};\textsc{s})}\lesssim h^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}|\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{C^{1}(\overline{J};\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\Omega))}+\ell_{\Omega}h^{\nu_{v}}|v|_{C^{1}(\overline{J};H^{1+\nu_{v}}(\Omega))}.

Moreover, if
\underline{v}\in C^{2}(\overline{J};\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\Omega)\times H^{1+\nu_{v}}(\Omega))
with
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}},\nu_{v}\in(\frac{1}{2},k+1]
, we have

\lVert\partial_{t}e^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{C^{0}(J_{t};\Omega)}\!+\!\lVert\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rVert_{C^{0}(J_{t};\Omega)}\!+\!|\partial_{t}\hat{e}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\mathcal{M}}|_{L^{2}(J_{t};\textsc{s})}\lesssim h^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}|\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{C^{2}(\overline{J};\boldsymbol{H}^{\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}(\Omega))}+\ell_{\Omega}h^{\nu_{v}}|v|_{C^{2}(\overline{J};H^{1+\nu_{v}}(\Omega))}.

Whenever
\nu_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\nu_{v}=k+1
, the above estimates converge optimally with rate
\mathcal{O}(h^{k+1})
.

Recall that
J_{t}:=(0,t)
for all
t\in\widetilde{J}
and set

\utilde{e}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t):=\int_{J_{t}}e_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(s)\,\mathrm{ds},\;\forall t\in\widetilde{J}.

Theorem 3.16

(Superconvergent
L^{2}
-error estimate for time-integrated primal variable)
.

Let
s\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
be the index of elliptic regularity pickup in
\Omega
. Let
\delta:=s
if
k^{\prime}=0
and
\delta:=0
if
k^{\prime}\geq 1
.
Assume that
\underline{v}_{0}\in\underline{D}^{\textsc{h}}
and
\underline{v}\in C^{1}(\overline{J};\underline{V})\cap C^{2}(\overline{J};\underline{L})
. The following holds:

\displaystyle\lVert\utilde{e}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}(T_{f})\rVert_{\Omega}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle h^{s}\ell_{\Omega}^{1-s}T_{f}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\{\ell_{\Omega}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lvert\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}\rvert_{L^{2}(J;\textsc{s})}+\ell_{\Omega}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lvert\partial_{t}\hat{e}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}\rvert_{L^{2}(J;\textsc{s})}+\lVert\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1229281}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1092269}{327680}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\textsc{HHO}\mathstrut$}}}}}}\rVert_{L^{2}(J;\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle+\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\nabla(\partial_{t}v-{\cal E}_{\mathcal{T}}^{k+1}(\partial_{t}v))\rVert_{L^{2}(J;\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(\Omega))}+\lVert\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}_{\mathcal{T}}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{L^{2}(J;\boldsymbol{L}^{2}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle+h^{1-\delta}\ell_{\Omega}^{\delta}\lVert\nabla{\cdot}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}(\nabla{\cdot}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma})\rVert_{L^{2}(J;L^{2}(\Omega))}\Big\}+\ell_{\Omega}\lVert\Pi_{\mathcal{T}}^{k^{\prime}}(v_{0})-\Pi^{v}_{\mathcal{T}}(\underline{v}_{0})\rVert_{\Omega}.

(3.15)

Remark 3.17

(Convergence rates)
.

Assume
